Established Name	fosnetupitant/ palonosetron
(Proposed) Trade Name	Akynzeo® for injection
Applicant	Helsinn Healthcare SA
Formulation(s)	235mg fosnetupitant/0.5 mg palonosetron lyophilized powder in single-dose vial for reconstitution
Dosing Regimen	1 vial of Akynzeo; infuse over 30 minutes starting 30 minutes before chemotherapy.
Applicant Proposed Indication(s)/Population(s)	AKYNZEO for injection is indicated for the prevention of acute and delayed nausea and vomiting associated with initial and repeat courses of highly emetogenic cancer chemotherapy.
AKYNZEO is a (b) (4) combination of palonosetron and netupitant (capsules) or fosnetupitant, a pro-drug of netupitant (for injection): palonosetron prevents nausea and vomiting during the acute phase and netupitant/fosnetupitant prevents nausea and vomiting during both the acute and delayed phase after cancer chemotherapy.
Recommendation on Regulatory Action	Approval
Recommended Indication(s)/Population(s) (if applicable)	AKYNZEO for injection is indicated in combination with dexamethasone in adults for the prevention of acute and delayed nausea and vomiting associated with initial and repeat courses of highly emetogenic cancer chemotherapy.
AKYNZEO for injection is a combination of palonosetron and fosnetupitant, a prodrug of netupitant: palonosetron prevents nausea and vomiting during the acute phase and fosnetupitant prevents nausea and vomiting during both the acute and delayed phase after cancer chemotherapy.
Limitations of Use
AKYNZEO for injection has not been studied for the prevention of nausea and vomiting associated with anthracycline plus cyclophosphamide chemotherapy.

L27: ## 1 Executive Summary
L28: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L29: Akynzeo® for injection is a combination antiemetic medication containing 235 mg of fosnetupitant (a NK1 antagonist) and 0.25 milligrams of palonosetron (a 5HT3 antagonist). This product is a lyophilized powder contained in a vial and is reconstituted in 50 milliliters of 5 percent Dextrose Injection, USP or 0.9 % Sodium Chloride Injection, USP.
L30: The proposed dosing recommendations are for administration of one vial as a 30-minute intravenous infusion starting 30 minutes prior to the start of chemotherapy. The proposed dosing recommendations also state that dexamethasone 12 mg should be administered 30 minutes prior to chemotherapy, followed by dexamethasone 8 mg once a day on Days 2 to 4.
L31: The proposed indication of Akynzeo for Injection is “for the prevention of acute and delayed nausea and vomiting associated with initial and repeat courses of highly emetogenic cancer chemotherapy”. It should be noted that the proposed indication also includes a statement that palonosetron prevents nausea and vomiting during the acute phase and fosnetupitant prevents nausea and vomiting during both the acute and delayed phase after cancer chemotherapy.
L32: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L33: The Clinical reviewer, CDTL, Division Director and ODE III Director all recommend approval of NDA 210493 for Akynzeo for injection (fosnetupitant and palonosetron fixed dose combination) for the indication: “AKYNZEO for injection is indicated in combination with dexamethasone in adults for the prevention of acute and delayed nausea and vomiting associated with initial and repeat courses of highly emetogenic cancer chemotherapy.”; the indication should include a statement that Akynzeo for injection is a fixed combination of palonosetron and fosnetupitant, a pro-drug of netupitant; and should include a statement that palonosetron prevents nausea and vomiting during the acute phase and fosnetupitant prevents nausea and vomiting during both the acute and delayed phase after cancer chemotherapy. Furthermore, the following Limitations of Use statement should be added: “AKYNZEO for injection has not been studied for the prevention of nausea and vomiting associated with anthracycline plus cyclophosphamide chemotherapy.”
L34: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L35: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L36: Akynzeo for injection (fosnetupitant and palonosetron fixed dose combination) is proposed for an indication in adults for the prevention of acute and delayed nausea and vomiting associated with initial and repeat courses of highly emetogenic chemotherapy or HEC. Akynzeo® for injection is recommended for approval with an indicated population of HEC not including anthracycline/cyclophosphamide (AC) based chemotherapy. The benefit of this product, when used in combination with dexamethasone, is to prevent chemotherapy induced nausea and vomiting (CINV) in this population.
L37: CINV diminishes the quality of life of cancer patients receiving HEC. The current standard of care is for patients about to receive HEC chemotherapy to receive both a 5-HT3 receptor antagonist and an NK1 receptor antagonist, in combination with dexamethasone. There are multiple medications with HEC indications (in both IV and oral dosage forms) that represent each of these drug classes; in addition, the oral dosage form of Akynzeo (netupitant and palonosetron) is approved with a HEC indication.
L38: The evidence for the effectiveness of Akynzeo for injection includes:
L39: - Studies that supported the approval of Akynzeo capsules: Akynzeo capsules are indicated for HEC including AC in both the acute and delayed phases. The effect of palonosetron was demonstrated in the acute phase, and the effect of netupitant was demonstrated in both the acute and delayed phases.
- Studies that supported the approval of Aloxi (palonosetron) IV: Aloxi (palonosetron) IV is indicated for HEC in the acute phase; and for moderately emetogenic chemotherapy (MEC) in both the acute and delayed phases. It should be noted that the studies supporting this indication were based on a former classification of HEC and MEC where HEC did not include AC, and MEC included AC.
- Studies submitted in the current NDA: These are described below.Non-Inferiority Trial (Palonosetron 30-minute vs. 30 second infusion; Study PALO 15-17): This was a non-inferiority (NI) study in the acute phase conducted in a HEC population that did not include patients treated with AC comparing palonosetron infused over 30 minutes (as in the proposed product) to palonosetron infused over 30 seconds (as approved for Aloxi IV). Non-inferiority was demonstrated for the Complete Response endpoint in the acute phase.
- Relative Bioavailability Study (IV fosnetupitant vs. oral netupitant; Study PNET 12-23): This was a relative bioavailability study in healthy subjects which included a comparison of 260 mg of IV fosnetupitant (a prodrug of netupitant) with 300 mg of oral netupitant (the dose of netupitant in the approved Akynzeo capsules). Bioequivalence criteria were met (for AUC0-120h and AUC0-∞).
L40: The effect of palonosetron infused over 30 minutes was demonstrated in the acute phase in the population of HEC not including AC, and the effect of IV fosnetupitant was demonstrated in the acute and delayed phases in the population of HEC not including AC. It should be noted that because palonosetron IV has a delayed phase indication in the AC population but palonosetron PO does not, demonstration of the contribution of fosnetupitant IV in the delayed phase in the AC population would be needed for an Akynzeo IV indication in the delayed phase in the AC population and MEC.
L41: The following additional study was conducted:
L42: - Active control study (Akynzeo for infusion vs. Akynzeo capsules; NEPA 15-18): This was an active control study (not designed as a NI trial) with four HEC not including AC cycles. The results suggested similar safety between the two arms.
L43: No new safety signals were identified as compared to approved Akynzeo capsules. No risk management strategies beyond product labeling are recommended.
L44: This product is expected to be of benefit to patients receiving HEC not including AC who cannot tolerate oral therapy; these are patients receiving the most emetogenic chemotherapy regimens. Furthermore, the product will provide both 5-HT3 and NK1 receptor antagonist therapy with the administration of a single medication.
L45: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	Chemotherapy induced nausea and vomiting (CINV) especially in patients receiving HEC has a significant impact on the quality of life of cancer patients who are undergoing treatment for their disease.
Akynzeo® for injection will provide significant benefit by preventing CINV in patients receiving HEC not including AC.

Current Treatment Options	There are multiple antiemetics with HEC indications (in both IV and oral dosage forms) that represent each of the drug classes in Akynzeo for injection (i.e., 5-HT3 receptor antagonist and NK1 receptor antagonist); in addition, the oral dosage form of Akynzeo (netupitant and palonosetron) is approved with a HEC indication.
Akynzeo® for injection offers additional benefit to the existing treatment options by providing an alternative to cancer patients who cannot tolerate oral medication. Further, per the most recent ASCO guidelines, patients receiving HEC should be treated with both 5HT3 and NK1 receptor antagonists to prevent CINV. As a fixed dose combination product, Akynzeo® for injection allows for these patients to be treated with a single infusion instead of two separate infusions of a 5HT3 inhibitor and another approved NK-1 inhibitor.

Benefit

(ii) Studies that supported the approval of Aloxi (palonosetron) IV: Aloxi (palonosetron) IV is indicated for HEC in the acute phase; and for moderately emetogenic chemotherapy (MEC) in both the acute and delayed phases. It should be noted that the studies supporting this indication were based on a former classification of HEC and MEC where HEC did not include AC, and MEC included AC.

Bioequivalence criteria were met (for AUC_{0-120h} and AUC_{0-\infty}).

L48: ## 1.4. Patient Experience Data
L49: Per the applicant:
L50: “The 21st Century Cures Act was enacted 13Dec2016, and the IV NEPA NDA was submitted 20 April 2017, a difference of 129 days. Title III of the Act (attached) states, “*Following the approval of an application that was submitted under section 505(b) of this Act [i.e., an NDA] or section 351(a) of the Public Health Service Act [i.e., certain biologic license applications] at least 180 days after the date of enactment of the 21st Century Cures Act, the Secretary [FDA] shall make public a brief statement regarding the patient experience data and related information, if any, submitted and reviewed as part of such application.*” [underline added]
L51: Since the 21st Century Cures Act was enacted 13Dec2016 and the IV NEPA NDA was submitted 20April2017, and the timeframe between these dates is 129 days, not the at least 180 days the law states above, the applicant is not required to submit patient experience data.
L52: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L53: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L54: Chemotherapy-induced nausea and vomiting (CINV), particularly nausea, is still cited by cancer patients as having the highest impact on their quality of life.1 Risk factors for CINV include young age, female sex, platinum- or anthracycline-based chemotherapy, nondrinker status, emesis in the earlier cycles of chemotherapy, and a previous history of motion/ morning sickness.1 Patients who are undergoing chemotherapy can experience nausea and vomiting in the acute phase (0-24 hours after chemotherapy initiation), the delayed phase (&gt;24-120 hours after chemotherapy administration), or the overall phase (0-120 hours after chemotherapy administration). The emetogenicity of the chemotherapy also has an impact on CINV. The medications included in highly emetogenic chemotherapy (HEC) include but are not limited to cisplatin, carmustine, cyclophosphamide, dacarbazine, mechlorethamine, and streptozotocin.2 In 2011, the American Society of Clinical Oncology (ASCO) reclassified anthracycline-cyclophosphamide (AC) chemotherapy combinations from moderately emetogenic chemotherapy (MEC) to HEC even though the likelihood of emesis on these medications without antiemetic prophylaxis was 85 % while the rest of the medications in HEC were 90% or greater.3 In general, the emetic risk associated with HEC is greater than or equal to 90%. For MEC, the
L55: 1 Lorusso, V. Management of chemotherapy-induced nausea and vomiting by risk profile: role of netupitant/palonosetron. *Therapeutics and Clinical Risk Management*. 2016 Jun 7;12:917-25. doi: 10.2147/TCRM.S89215. eCollection 2016.
L56: 2 Hesketh, P.J. *et al.* Antiemetics: American Society of Clinical Oncology Clinical Practice Guideline Update. *Journal of Clinical Oncology*. 2017 35:28, 3240-3261.
L57: 3 www.asco.org/guidelines/antiemetics
L58: emetic risk is from 30-90 percent.
L59: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L60: With respect to CINV, there are two main classes of medications that are used: neurokinin 1 (NK_1) receptor antagonists and serotonin (5-HT_3) receptor antagonists. 5-HT_3 antagonists are thought to work in the acute phase whereas NK_1 inhibitors are thought to work in both the acute phase and the delayed phase. The only exception to this is IV palonosetron which has an indication for delayed phase MEC.
L61: The 2017 ASCO guidelines recommend that adult patients who are treated with cisplatin and other high-emetic-risk single agents should be offered the four-drug combination of a NK_1 receptor antagonist, a 5-HT_3 receptor antagonist, dexamethasone, and olanzapine. As per these guidelines, dexamethasone and olanzapine should be continued on days 2 to 4.1 Adult patients who are treated with an anthracycline combined with cyclophosphamide should be offered a four-drug combination of an NK_1 receptor antagonist, a 5-HT_3 receptor antagonist, dexamethasone, and olanzapine. Olanzapine should be continued on days 2 to 4.1 It should be noted that olanzapine is not approved for this indication. The basis of the recommendation in the 2017 ASCO guidelines is a phase 3 randomized controlled trial which suggested that adding olanzapine to antiemetic prophylaxis (dexamethasone, aprepitant or fosaprepitant, and a 5-hydroxytryptamine type 3-receptor antagonist) reduces the likelihood of nausea among adult patients who are treated with high emetic risk antineoplastic agents (patients with no previous chemotherapy who were receiving cisplatin or cyclophosphamide-doxorubicin).4 Although olanzapine has not been included in the designs of studies by commercial sponsors for this indication (i.e., “in combination with dexamethasone in adults for the prevention of acute and delayed nausea and vomiting associated with initial and repeat courses of highly emetogenic cancer chemotherapy”), we anticipate that in the future, sponsors will propose that olanzapine also be included in these study designs. Dexamethasone, which currently appears in the dosage and administration instructions in the labels of approved antiemetics as a component of a combination regimen for prevention of chemotherapy induced nausea and vomiting (CINV), also does not have a CINV indication in its product labeling.
L62: The first 5-HT_3 inhibitor, intravenous ondansetron was approved in January 1991. Its indication statement did not address HEC or MEC: “prevention of nausea and vomiting associated with initial and repeat courses of emetogenic cancer chemotherapy.” Oral ondansetron was approved in December 1992, and the indication statement did contain language regarding HEC and MEC.
L63: Intravenous granisetron was approved in 1993, and its indication statement contained general language (i.e., “emetogenic cancer therapy”); however, there was also a qualifier included, “including high dose cisplatin.” The Clinical Studies section also had a subsection called “moderately emetogenic chemotherapy study” that specifically described MEC as carboplatin, cisplatin 20-50 mg/m^2, and cyclophosphamide &gt;600 mg/m^2.
L64: Oral dolasetron was approved in September 1997 with the indication “prevention of nausea and
L65: 4 Navari RM, Qin R, Ruddy KJ et al., “Olanzapine for the Prevention of Chemotherapy-Induced Nausea and Vomiting”, N Engl J Med 2016; 375:134-142.
L66: vomiting associated with moderately emetogenic cancer chemotherapy, including high dose cisplatin.” The NDA had a single MEC study which was described under the header “Cyclophosphamide Based Chemotherapy” and describes the study as primarily enrolling women receiving “moderately emetogenic chemotherapy such as cyclophosphamide based regimens.”
L67: Intravenous palonosetron was approved in July 2003 with indications specifically identifying HEC and MEC. Oral palonosetron was approved in 2008 with an indication limited to MEC. Due to its long half-life, the clinical development plans for both the intravenous and oral versions of this drug included evaluations in the acute and delayed phases. IV palonosetron was demonstrated to show efficacy in the acute and delayed phases of MEC (the majority of the patients received anthracycline plus cyclophosphamide chemotherapy, approximately 60%), but was only shown to be efficacious in the acute phase of HEC. Oral palonosetron has no indication for the delayed phase as oral palonosetron 0.5 mg was not found to be non-inferior to IV palonosetron 0.25 mg.
L68: The treatments for CINV are summarized in Table 1. The regulatory significance will be further discussed in section 3.

L73: ## 3 Regulatory Background
L74: ## 3.1. U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L75: Akynzeo® for injection is proposed as a fixed dose combination product containing fosnetupitant and palonosetron. Akynzeo® capsules (netupitant + palonosetron HCl), which is currently marketed as a fixed dose combination product, was approved in October 2014 (NDA 205718) for the following indication:
L76: “AKYNZEO is indicated for the prevention of acute and delayed nausea and vomiting associated with initial and repeat courses of cancer chemotherapy, including, but not limited to, highly emetogenic chemotherapy.”
L77: In addition to being marketed in this country, oral Akynzeo® is also currently being sold abroad (Europe).
L78: The applicant's proposed indication is for the acute and delayed phase of HEC. There have been no other indications proposed.
L79: ## 3.2. Summary of Presubmission/Submission Regulatory Activity
L80: In 2011, ASCO reclassified anthracycline-cyclophosphamide (AC) chemotherapy combinations from MEC to HEC even though the likelihood of emesis on these medications without antiemetic prophylaxis was 85% while the rest of the medications in HEC were 90% or greater.3 This created several challenges for the Division from a regulatory perspective, in considering the appropriate indication (HEC/MEC) for programs in which the MEC development had focused on patients receiving AC combination chemotherapy. Traditionally, clinical trials designed to obtain MEC indications were designed using patients on anthracycline-cyclophosphamide chemotherapy combinations, predominantly breast cancer patients. In general, females have not been as responsive to antiemetic prophylaxis in the setting of CINV. After reviewing the regulatory history and development programs for products with MEC and/or HEC indications, the Division concluded there was a lack of persuasive evidence that a product effective for HEC would not also be effective for MEC. It seemed reasonable to conclude that if a product was shown to be effective to prevent CINV in the setting of cisplatin based HEC, efficacy would also be expected in the setting of less emetogenic chemotherapy. The Division noted that given the differential treatment effect of intravenous palonosetron in CINV delayed phase between the HEC and MEC settings (it was effective for CINV delayed phase in the setting of MEC, in which 70-80% of patients studied received AC, but was not shown to be effective in CINV delayed phase after cisplatin based HEC, a clinical development program that focuses on HEC, targeting
L81: a general indication (“prevention of CINV, including but not limited to HEC”) should study cisplatin based HEC separately from AC.
L82: A further regulatory consideration involving Akynzeo® for injection involves the Combination Rule. Recalling that Akynzeo® is a combination of palonosetron and netupitant, it was necessary to show that both components of the drug were safe and effective. During the oral Akynzeo® development program, there were two trials designed to show the effect of netupitant, NETU 10-29 and NETU 08-18 (originally designed as a MEC trial) which compared Akynzeo® to oral palonosetron in AC chemotherapy. These two trials established the efficacy of netupitant for acute and delayed phase CINV in the setting of cisplatin based HEC and in the setting of AC combination chemotherapy. An additional trial, PALO 10-01, established noninferiority of oral palonosetron to IV palonosetron in prevention of acute phase of CINV in the setting of HEC. Oral palonosetron does not have an indication for the delayed phase of MEC; however, intravenous palonosetron does. This difference in established efficacy between the oral and palonosetron impacts the conclusions that can be drawn from the clinical development program submitted to support approval of the switch from the oral combination product to an intravenous combination product. For the purposes of the Combination Rule, the effect that oral Akynzeo® had in the delayed phase could be entirely attributed to the effect of the netupitant, given the lack of evidence of efficacy in the delayed phase associated with the oral palonosetron component of the combination. The switch to an intravenous palonosetron component of the combination necessitates a trial to establish that the intravenous netupitant component contributes to efficacy in the delayed phase in the setting of MEC, given that intravenous palonosetron has been shown to be effective for delayed phase of MEC. In the absence of trials to establish the contribution of netupitant to the intravenous fixed combination in the delayed phase of MEC, the applicant proposed an indication stating that it had never been studied in AC chemotherapy. The reference to AC chemotherapy acknowledges the majority of patients enrolled in the clinical trials that established the efficacy of intravenous palonosetron in delayed phase of MEC were treated with AC combination chemotherapy.
L83: Akynzeo® capsules were approved on October 10, 2014. Prior to this date, the IV and oral Akynzeo® development programs had been running in parallel. The following table summarizes the trials that were evaluated in the oral Akynzeo® development program. For more information, please refer to the oral Akynzeo® clinical review.

L87: The design of the Akynzeo® for injection program was based on a two-fold approach: 1) bridging to the approved Akynzeo capsules through establishing bioequivalence of the netupitant component, and 2) establishing, through a noninferiority trial design, that prolonging the infusion time of palonosetron from 30 seconds (the approved labeled dosing instructions) to 30 minutes retains palonosetron's efficacy. Safety of the intravenous formulation and fosnetupitant would be evaluated in the bioequivalence trial and in a repeat dosing trial. The key meetings between the FDA and Helsinn where the critical agreements on the Akynzeo for injection development program are summarized in the table below and the text that follows. Based on the FDA's advice after negotiation, the ultimate requirements for the IV Akynzeo (also referred to as IV NEPA FDC) clinical development program should include a bioequivalence trial (PNET-12-23), a non-inferiority (NI) efficacy trial on the palonosetron component (PALO-15-17), a clinical safety trial (NEPA-15-18) and two clinical pharmacology trials (i.e., PNET-13-63, drug-drug interactions in healthy volunteers; NEPA-15-19, PK in cancer patients).

L140: ## 4.2. Product Quality
L141: Akynzeo (fosnetupitant and palonosetron) for Injection, is a lyophilized powder intended for reconstitution and parenteral administration with either 5% dextrose or 0.9% sodium chloride. The lyophilized cake is reconstituted with 20 mL of diluent and the entire constituted solution is removed from the vial and further diluted up to 50 mL for administration.
L142: Each vial contains 235 mg of fosnetupitant (corresponding to 260 mg/vial of fosnetupitant chloride hydrochloride), 0.25 mg of palonosetron (equivalent to 0.28 mg/vial of palonosetron hydrochloride) and the excipients EDTA (b)(4) mannitol (b)(4), and NaOH or HCl as a (b)(4) fill excess in each vial in accordance with USP &lt;1151&gt; to permit withdrawal and to achieve the administration of the labeled dose of both actives. See figures below for the chemical structure of the fosnetupitant chloride and palonosetron.

L158: ## 4.4. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L159: The 4 week IV combination fosnetupitant + palonosetron study in dogs showed QTc prolongation at both mid and high doses (fosnetupitant/palonosetron = 3.95/3 mg/kg and 13.16/10 mg/kg) but this was attributed to palonosetron as it was seen at a dose level of 1 mg/kg in the palonosetron dog study (NDA 21372).
L160: Fosnetupitant was negative in the Ames test, *in vitro* chromosome aberration test in human lymphocytes, and the *in vivo* rat micronucleus test.
L161: In an embryo-fetal development study in rats, intravenous administration of 39.47 mg/kg/day fosnetupitant (3 times the human AUC for netupitant at the recommended single dose to be given with each cycle of chemotherapy) produced delayed ossification of pubis. No effects on embryo-fetal development were observed with IV administration of up to 13.16 mg/kg/day fosnetupitant in rats (2 times the human AUC for netupitant). In a rabbit embryo-fetal development study, an increase in resorptions was observed with intravenous administration of fosnetupitant at 6.25 mg/kg/day and higher in rabbits (9 times the human AUC for fosnetupitant and 0.4 times the human AUC for netupitant at the recommended single dose to be given with each cycle of chemotherapy). No effects were observed in rabbits at 3.13 mg/kg/day (5.4 times the human AUC for fosnetupitant and 0.4 times the human AUC for netupitant). In rabbits, the embryo-fetal effects of intravenous fosnetupitant differed from the effects of oral netupitant, and this difference can be attributed to the minimal transformation of fosnetupitant to netupitant in rabbits, in contrast to the rapid metabolism of fosnetupitant in rats and humans.
L162: In a pre-/postnatal study in rats, intravenous administration of 39.47 mg/kg/day fosnetupitant in rats (3 times the human AUC for netupitant) produced lower bodyweight in offspring at birth through maturation, and delayed physical development (pinna detachment, eye opening, and preputial separation). These effects were associated with maternal toxicity (reduced weight gain and food consumption). No effects occurred in offspring or dams at 13.16 mg/kg/day (2 times the human AUC for netupitant).
L163: Fosnetupitant produced no effects on fertility or early embryonic development in rats, at doses up to 39.47 mg/kg IV.
L164: ## 4.5. Clinical Pharmacology
L165: ## 4.5.1. Mechanism of Action
L166: Netupitant is a selective antagonist of human substance P/neurokinin 1 (NK1) receptors. Fosnetupitant is converted to the active drug, netupitant, through hydrolysis by non-CYP450 enzymes in liver and extrahepatic tissues.
L167: Palonosetron is a 5-HT3 receptor antagonist with a strong binding affinity for this receptor and little or no affinity for other receptors. Cancer chemotherapy may be associated with a high incidence of nausea and vomiting, particularly when certain agents, such as cisplatin, are used. 5-HT3 receptors are located on the nerve terminals of the vagus in the periphery and centrally in
L168: the chemoreceptor trigger zone of the area postrema. Chemotherapeutic agents produce nausea and vomiting by stimulating the release of serotonin from the enterochromaffin cells of the small intestine. Serotonin then activates 5-HT3 receptors located on vagal afferents to initiate the vomiting reflex. The development of acute emesis is known to depend on serotonin and its binding to 5-HT3 receptors have been demonstrated to selectively stimulate the emetic response.
L169: Delayed emesis has been largely associated with the activation of tachykinin family neurokinin 1 (NK1) receptors (broadly distributed in the central and peripheral nervous systems) by substance P. As shown in *in vitro* and *in vivo* studies, netupitant inhibits substance P mediated responses.
L170: ## 4.5.2. Pharmacodynamics
L171: ## NK1 Receptor Occupancy
L172: The receptor occupancy of netupitant was measured in a human positron emission tomography (PET) study. Netupitant was shown to cross the blood brain barrier with a NK1 receptor occupancy of 92.5%, 86.5%, 85.0%, 78.0%, and 76.0% in striatum at 6, 24, 48, 72, and 96 hours, respectively, after oral administration of 300 mg netupitant.5
L173: ## Cardiac Electrophysiology
L174: An oral dose 2 times the recommended dose of netupitant (600 mg) and 3 times the recommended dose of palonosetron (1.5 mg) in an AKYNZEO capsule, did not prolong the QT interval to any clinically relevant extent.
L175: The recommended dose of AKYNZEO for injection (235 mg fosnetupitant and 0.25 mg palonosetron), did not prolong the QT interval to any clinically relevant extent.
L176: ## 4.5.3. Pharmacokinetics
L177: ## Akynzeo for Injection
L178: After single dose administration of AKYNZEO for injection (235 mg fosnetupitant and 0.25 mg palonosetron) administered as a 30-minute infusion to cancer patients, fosnetupitant achieved Cmax at the end of the infusion with an apparent terminal half-life less than 1 hour. Within 30 minutes of completion of the infusion, the concentration of fosnetupitant decreased to less than 1% of the Cmax.

L182: 5 Content adapted from approved label
L183: **Fosnetupitant 260 mg IV vs. Netupitant 300 mg PO:** The relative bioavailability (260 mg IV fosnetupitant vs. 300 mg oral netupitant) is discussed in the sub-sections below (“Relative Bioavailability (260 mg IV fosnetupitant vs. 300 mg oral netupitant)” and “Geometric Means of Netupitant Metabolites: IV versus Oral”).
L184: **Palonosetron 30 minutes IV vs. Palonosetron 30 seconds IV:** The Clinical Pharmacology Review noted the following (***emphasis added***): “Because Akynzeo IV is administered over 30 minutes while Aloxi is administered via IV push over 30 seconds, the ***Cmax of Aloxi will be higher than that from Akynzeo IV*** while AUC will be comparable because the dose is the same. Therefore, ***safety of the 30-minute infusion of palonosetron is at least comparable if not better than Aloxi administered by IV push***. On the other hand, the sponsor ***conducted a pivotal phase 3 study to evaluate the potential difference in efficacy due to the lower Cmax*** and demonstrated that the efficacy of palonosetron IV administered over 30 minutes is not inferior to Aloxi injectable.” See Section 6.1 of this review for a discussion of the non-inferiority study (PALO-15-17) comparing palonosetron IV administered over 30 minutes to Aloxi IV administered over 30 seconds.
L185: ## **Relative Bioavailability (260 mg IV fosnetupitant vs. 300 mg oral netupitant)**
L186: The relative bioavailability study (PNET 12-23) included a comparison of 260 mg of IV fosnetupitant with 300 mg of oral netupitant. Bioequivalence criteria were met for AUC_{0-12h} and AUC_{0-\infty} for the comparison of 260 mg of IV fosnetupitant (a prodrug of netupitant) with 300 mg of oral netupitant (the dose of netupitant in the approved Akynzeo capsules). The Cmax for 260 mg IV fosnetupitant was approximately 1.5 times the Cmax for 300 mg PO netupitant, and exceeds the BE criteria range. Please see table below.

L190: Assuming the efficacy of netupitant is based solely on netupitant levels (see discussion of netupitant major metabolites below), these BE study results indicate that the efficacy of the oral netupitant and the intravenous fosnetupitant component of the two products can be expected to be comparable. However, the safety of the higher netupitant Cmax associated with the intravenous product must be addressed. The safety was evaluated in Study NEPA 15-18 (see section 8 of this review).
L191: ## **Geometric Means of Netupitant Metabolites: IV versus Oral**
L192: The Clinical Pharmacology Reviewer compared the geometric means (AUC_{0-120h}) of netupitant's metabolites (M1, M2, and M3) for IV fosnetupitant versus oral netupitant, and found that the metabolites were lower with IV fosnetupitant (29%, 24%, and 16% lower, respectively) than orally administered netupitant. Similarly, the intravenous product Cmax of each netupitant metabolite was lower than the oral product (39%, 34% and 25% lower, respectively). See table below.

L201: The Clinical Pharmacology Reviewer questioned whether lower metabolite exposure might negatively impact the efficacy of the proposed IV product relative to the oral product. The clinical program submitted to support this NDA did not include an efficacy trial with the primary objective to compare efficacy of fosnetupitant vs. netupitant based on a clinical efficacy endpoint. The objective of Study NEPA 15-18 was to evaluate the safety of the product relative to the approved oral product. It was not designed as a noninferiority trial and was insufficiently powered to answer a comparative efficacy question. However, efficacy was measured in the trial and the Clinical Pharmacology reviewers noted that the observed Complete Response in the Delayed Phase was numerically lower in the IV product arm than in the oral product arm (see Section 6.2.2).
L202: To address the issue of whether the lower metabolite levels are clinically meaningful, the review team evaluated the evidence to support whether each of these metabolites is active and contributes to the efficacy of netupitant. The pharmacology/toxicology reviewers noted the following:
L203: - The three major metabolites, M1 (Ro 68-1133), M2 (Ro 71-3001), and M3 (Ro 73-1519), also bind to the hNK1 receptor, with pKi values of 9.0, 9.0, and 9.1, respectively.
- Netupitant inhibited NK1 receptor agonist-induced foot-tapping behavior in gerbils in a dose-dependent manner. The metabolites M1, M2, and M3, also blocked the foot-tapping activity in this study. However, the relevance of the effect on foot-tapping activity to the pharmacodynamic activity for the proposed indication (anti-emesis) is unknown.
L204: The pharmacology/toxicology reviewers noted that these nonclinical data do not rule in or rule out a substantive role of these metabolites in the product's efficacy in humans. Given these limitations, the Clinical Pharmacology reviewers further considered available human PK and efficacy data that could inform the question. They identified the dose-ranging trial (Study NETU-07-07) submitted in the NDA for oral Akynzeo capsules as an important resource for addressing the question. This trial was the major trial relied upon to establish the efficacy of oral netupitant in the setting of HEC. The efficacy results of Study NETU-07-07 are summarized in the table below, which is reproduced from Dr. Yeh-Fong Chen's statistical review for NDA 205718. See below.

As noted in the Statistical review for the current application, the Statistical reviewers evaluated the results of Study NETU-07-07 and concluded that the data support that oral netupitant 100 mg in combination with palonosetron 0.5 mg is superior to palonosetron 0.5 mg for prevention of CINV in the delayed phase. They also concluded the following regarding the comparisons of the three netupitant dose combinations (with a static palonosetron dose) to palonosetron alone in Study NETU-07-07: “the smallest p-value 0.008 for the comparison between PALO+NETU 200mg and PALO alone is less than 0.05/3, second smallest p-value 0.016 for the comparison between PALO+NETU 100mg and PALO alone is less than 0.05/2 and the largest p-value 0.017 for the comparison between PALO+NETU 300mg and PALO alone is less than 0.05. Based on the Holm-Bonferroni method to control the overall type I error rate due to the three study doses, we can conclude that statistically significant superiority to palonosetron alone was observed for each dose of netupitant.”

L212: Given that this trial supported the effectiveness of the 100 mg netupitant dose, the Clinical Pharmacology reviewers identified pharmacokinetic data associated with that dose level to evaluate how the major metabolite levels at that dose compare to the metabolite levels associated with fosnetupitant at the dose proposed for approval. The Clinical Pharmacology Reviewer
L213: noted 260 mg IV fosnetupitant (from studies PNET 12-23 in healthy subjects, PNET 13-63 in healthy subjects, and NEPA 15-19 in cancer patients) is associated with considerably higher M1, M2, and M3 levels (as measured by AUC_{0-120h}) than those associated with 100 mg PO netupitant (from study NP16003 in cancer patients): M1 = 2 to 6 times higher, M2 = 6 to 7.5 times higher, and M3 = 2 to 2.7 times higher. It should be noted that the Clinical Pharmacology Reviewer also presented data from Study NETU-09-07 (in healthy subjects) and Study NETU-10-09 (in cancer patients) which suggest that PK is reasonably similar between healthy subjects and cancer patients.
L214: **Conclusion:** These results indicate that the lower M1, M2, and M3 exposures (260 mg IV vs. 300 mg PO, see tables above) would not be expected to result in lower efficacy in the delayed phase (Akynzeo IV vs. Akynzeo PO), as the M1, M2, and M3 exposures for fosnetupitant 260 mg IV do not fall below the exposures seen with the lowest effective netupitant PO dose for the delayed phase (100 mg) evaluated in Study NETU-07-07.
L215: ## QT Evaluation
L216: The reader is referred to the QT-IRT Consult Review for complete information. The QT-IRT concluded that no significant QTc prolongation effect of intravenous administration by 30-minute infusion of IV NEPA FDC (260 mg fosnetupitant7 and 0.25 mg palonosetron) is expected based on the totality of the evidence that includes the results from the following four clinical studies (two TQT studies and two studies of IV NEPA FDC):
L217: - A TQT study for IV palonosetron (Aloxi®) showed no clinically significant effect on the QTc interval with a single supratherapeutic dose of 2.25 mg palonosetron IV short infusion. This dose level provides &gt;9-fold exposure margin over the exposures anticipated with therapeutic dosing of 0.25 mg palonosetron given as a 30 min IV infusion in IV NEPA FDC product. (The palonosetron Cmax associated with the currently approved 30 second infusion is higher than that associated with the proposed 30-minute infusion.)
- A TQT study for Oral NEPA FDC (Akynzeo®; 300 mg netupitant and 0.5 mg palonosetron) showed no clinically significant effect on the QTc interval with single oral supratherapeutic dose of 600 mg netupitant and 1.5 mg palonosetron. The QT-IRT review states that the 600 mg dose of oral netupitant provides similar exposure to netupitant from IV NEPA FDC. Specifically, the netupitant AUC of the proposed IV product is bioequivalent to the AUC of the currently marketed oral product; however, the Cmax is 50% higher (IV/oral ratio = 1.51; 90% CI: 1.33, 1.73). Therefore, the netupitant Cmax associated with the 600 mg dose of oral netupitant is similar, if not somewhat lower, than that associated with the proposed IV fosnetupitant product. The QT-IRT review states that the maximum dose evaluated in the previous TQT study of oral netupitant does not cover the worst case scenario of potential increased netupitant exposure expected with hepatic impairment (1.4- fold increase), but points to the lack of QT interval prolongation associated with the intravenous fosnetupitant dose of 390 mg, which was evaluated in Study PNET-12-23 (described in the next bullet), as
L218: 7 Equivalent to 235 mg fosnetupitant free-base
L219: evidence that this worst case scenario has been adequately addressed in the development program.
L220: - The bioequivalence trial comparing oral netupitant to intravenous fosnetupitant, Study PNET-12-23, included a fosnetupitant dose escalation phase that evaluated a maximum fosnetupitant dose of 390 mg and ECG monitoring associated with PK sampling. It was a single center, randomized, phase 1, double-blind, active-controlled, parallel group study, in which 160 healthy subjects were enrolled: 148 subjects received single ascending IV fosnetupitant (doses from 19.5 mg up to 390 mg with N=8-30 subjects in different dose-groups), and 134 subjects received single dose of oral NEPA FDC. No large (&gt;20 ms) mean increases in baseline-corrected QTcF were observed in the central tendency analysis8. None of the subjects in the 260 mg IV fosnetupitant dose group (N=30) had QTcF &gt;450 ms or \DeltaQTcF &gt;30 ms post-dose. Fosnetupitant is a new molecular entity and has not been studied in any prior TQT studies. The nonclinical cardiac safety studies did not identify any potential issues for fosnetupitant. The 1.5-fold exposure margin provided by the 390 mg IV fosnetupitant dose in this study over the 260 mg therapeutic dose proposed for approval adequately covers the highest clinically relevant exposure of fosnetupitant because fosnetupitant clearance (conversion to netupitant) is likely mediated by non-saturable enzymes, such as phosphatases and esterases, distributed throughout the body and none of the intrinsic/extrinsic factors are expected to increase its exposure. Within this exposure margin, the C-QTc assessment did not show a positive relationship for fosnetupitant.
- Study NEPA-15-18: 202 subjects received oral NEPA FDC and 203 subjects received IV NEPA FDC. No large (&gt;20 ms) mean increases in baseline-corrected QTcF were observed in the central tendency analysis9. Similar values for outliers of QTcF and \DeltaQTcF were seen for the two study arms (IV NEPA FDC and oral NEPA FDC). The cardiac safety profile was also similar between these two study arms.
L221: Furthermore, the sponsor has stated that “oral Akynzeo (oral NEPA FDC) has been marketed for 2 years and after more than 14,000 patient exposures with more than 85,000 doses, no QTc reactions have been reported in the post marketing surveillance data.”
L222: The considerations for exposures and QTc effects for the active drug (netupitant) and metabolite moieties (M1, M2, and M3), that are formed from the pro-drug fosnetupitant, are detailed below:
L223: **Netupitant:** The C_{max} of fosnetupitant and netupitant occur at the end of the 30-minute infusion of IV NEPA FDC and mean concentrations of netupitant remained at nearly the same level until 4 h post-infusion. The netupitant C_{max} observed with the therapeutic dosing of IV NEPA FDC is ~2-fold of that after oral 300 mg netupitant (823 vs. 421 \mug/L), but is similar to the netupitant C_{max} observed with supratherapeutic dose (600 mg) of netupitant used in TQT study of oral Akynzeo. Although the previous TQT study of oral Akynzeo does not cover the worst case scenario of possible increase in netupitant exposure up to 1.4-fold that could be expected with hepatic impairment, these exposures are covered by the 390 mg IV fosnetupitant (1.5-fold the proposed therapeutic dose of 260 mg fosnetupitant in IV NEPA FDC) that is studied in PNET-
L224: 8 Because there were no placebo and positive controls for QT evaluation in both studies (PNET-12-23 and NEPA-15-18), the data can be used to exclude only large mean QTc effects (20 ms).
L225: 9 Because there were no placebo and positive controls for QT evaluation in both studies (PNET-12-23 and NEPA-15-18), the data can be used to exclude only large mean QTc effects (20 ms).
L226: 12-23, and data from this study did not show a positive C-QTc relationship for netupitant. These results are consistent with the prior TQT study where no positive C-QTc relationship was identified for netupitant.
L227: **Netupitant Metabolites M1, M2 and M3:** The intensive PK sampling in Study PNET-12-23 showed that the C_{max} for netupitant metabolites M1, M2 and M3 are around 12 h (steady conc. from 8 to 24 h), 2-3 h and 24 h, respectively. Although the ECG sampling in the two studies (PNET-12-23 and NEPA-15-18) was not adequate to capture the effects near T_{max} of these metabolites, these metabolite exposures with therapeutic dosing of IV NEPA FDC were well covered by the supratherapeutic dose of 600 mg netupitant in the TQT study for the approved oral NEPA FDC (2-fold [54 vs. 26 \mug/L], 1.5-fold [228 vs. 156 \mug/L] and 1.9-fold [102 vs. 53 \mug/L] exposure margin for M1, M2 and M3, respectively). Furthermore, there was no statistically significant positive C-QTc relationship seen with each of these 3 metabolites in the prior TQT study for oral Akynzeo. So, further exploration of C-QTc relationships for these metabolites was not carried out in this review and the lack of ECG sampling near T_{max} for these metabolites in the submitted studies (PNET-12-23 and NEPA-15-18) does not impact the overall conclusion.
L228: ## Effects of Akynzeo IV on S-warfarin
L229: The applicant submitted data from evaluations of the impact of fosnetupitant on transporters and CYP450 enzymes, and vice versa. The reviewers have recommended labeling revisions based on this information. Although modeling suggested that intravenous Akynzeo would not have a substantial impact on S-warfarin (a CYP2C9 substrate), the reviewers recommended inclusion of cautionary language in the label. The applicant did not evaluate INR changes in drug-drug interaction studies. The label will state:
L230: ## Warfarin
L231: Although it was predicted that co-administration of intravenous AKYNZEO with warfarin would not substantially increase the systemic exposure to S-warfarin (CYP2C9 substrate), the active enantiomer, the effects of AKYNZEO for injection and AKYNZEO capsules on INR and prothrombin time have not been studied. Monitor INR and adjust the dosage of warfarin, as needed with concomitant use of AKYNZEO, to maintain the target INR range.

L248: ## 5.2. Review Strategy
L249: ## Efficacy
L250: This review will focus primarily on evaluation of efficacy data from the non-inferiority trial (Study PALO 15-17). In particular, the trial will be reviewed for demonstration of non-inferiority (IV palonosetron 0.25 mg over 30 minutes vs. palonosetron 0.25 mg over 30 seconds) for the primary endpoint of Complete Response (CR) in the acute phase after a single chemotherapy cycle. Study PNET 12-23, the relative bioavailability study which bridges the proposed intravenous product to the approved oral product, based on netupitant exposure, and establishes the efficacy of fosnetupitant for the delayed (and acute) phase of CINV associated with HEC, was presented above in Section 4.5 Clinical Pharmacology.
L251: The descriptive efficacy analyses from Study NEPA 15-18 (Akynzeo IV vs. Akynzeo capsules) will be reviewed and presented for completeness; but these data are not critical to the evaluation of efficacy as the trial was not designed/powered to demonstrate non-inferiority.
L252: ## Safety
L253: Safety data from Studies PALO 15-17 and NEPA 15-18 will be reviewed. In addition, safety data from Study PNET 12-23 (which included a relative bioavailability comparison of fosnetupitant 260 mg IV vs. netupitant 300 mg PO) will be reviewed. Key differences between
L254: the products that required randomized, controlled safety assessment in this program include: 1) fosnetupitant is a NME, 2) the netupitant Cmax associated with the IV product exceeds that of the oral product, and 3) the palonosetron infusion duration is prolonged relative to currently marketed product's dosage and administration labeling.
L255: ## General Approach
L256: This review will present some analyses performed by the applicant (with the Clinical reviewer's commentary) and some of the Clinical Reviewer's own analyses. These analyses were performed using applicant datasets and the JReview software tool. Tables in which the data are presented will clearly identify the source of the data analyzed.
L257: ## 6 Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
L258: ## 6.1. PALO 15-17
L259: ## 6.1.1. Study Design
L260: ## Overview and Objective
L261: PALO 15-17 was titled "A phase 3, single-dose, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, parallel group study to assess the efficacy and safety of palonosetron 0.25 mg administered as a 30-minute IV infusion compared to palonosetron 0.25 mg administered as a 30-second IV bolus for the prevention of chemotherapy-induced nausea and vomiting in cancer patients receiving highly emetogenic chemotherapy."
L262: PALO 15-17 was designed to provide evidence that a 30-minute infusion of IV palonosetron 0.25 mg was non-inferior to the 30-second bolus injection of palonosetron 0.25 mg (the approved posology for Aloxi IV) in the acute phase of CINV. The primary objective of the study was to demonstrate non-inferiority of a single IV dose of palonosetron 0.25 mg administered as a 30-minute infusion versus a single dose of palonosetron 0.25 mg administered as a 30 second IV bolus with oral dexamethasone in terms of a complete response in the acute phase.
L263: ***Reviewer's Comments:** PALO 15-17 was necessary to establish that the longer palonosetron infusion time associated with the proposed product (relative to the approved palonosetron infusion time) does not negatively impact the efficacy of palonosetron in the acute phase of CINV.*
L264: ## Trial Design
L265: ## Overview
L266: PALO 15-17 was a phase 3, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, parallel group, non-inferiority, stratified study assessing the efficacy, safety, and tolerability of a single dose of palonosetron 0.25 mg administered as a 30-minute IV infusion versus a single dose of
L267: palonosetron 0.25 mg administered as a 30-second IV bolus, each administered with oral dexamethasone prior to HEC. It was designed to focus on the acute phase of CINV. (Note that the treatment effect of intravenous palonosetron for CINV was established in the clinical trial in which it was given in combination with dexamethasone.)
L268: ***Reviewer's Comments:*** *There were no significant weaknesses to this study. Its design was consistent with prior agreements between the Sponsor and the FDA (please see section 3.2).*
L269: ## Number of Patients
L270: The number of patients to be randomized into the study was 440 equally distributed in the two treatment groups (i.e., 220 patients/group).
L271: ## Pertinent Inclusion Criteria
L272: - Signed written informed consent.
- Male or female patient \ge 18 years of age.
- Histologically or cytologically confirmed solid tumor malignancy.
- Naïve to cytotoxic chemotherapy. Previous biological or hormonal therapy is permitted.
- Scheduled to receive first course of one of the following reference HEC, alone or in combination with other chemotherapeutic agents* on Day 1:cisplatin administered as a single IV dose of \ge 70 mg/m2
- cyclophosphamide \ge 1500 mg/m2
- carmustine (BCNU) &gt; 250 mg/m2
- dacarbazine (DTIC)
- mechloretamine (nitrogen mustard)
L273: The administration of the reference HEC should not extend beyond 4 hours on study Day 1.
L274: *on Day 1, additional HEC or MEC chemotherapeutic agents have to be administered after the start of the reference HEC administration and their administration must be completed no more than 6 hours after the start of reference HEC infusion. Low, minimally, or not emetogenic chemotherapies can be administered at any time after the start of the reference HEC.10
L275: - ECOG Performance Status of 0, 1, or 2.
- If a patient is female, she shall be: a) of non-childbearing potential or b) of childbearing potential using reliable contraceptive measures and having negative urine pregnancy test.
- Hematologic and metabolic status adequate for receiving a HEC regimen and fulfillment of the following criteria:Total Neutrophils \ge 1500/mm3 (Standard units: \ge 1.5 x 109/L)
- Platelets \ge 100,000/mm3 (Standard units: \ge 100.0 x 109/L)
- Bilirubin \le 1.5 x Upper Limit of Normal (ULN)
- Liver enzymes:
L276: 10 MASCC/ESMO Antiemetic Guideline 2013; Guideline update for MASCC and ESMO in the prevention of chemotherapy- and radiotherapy-induced nausea and vomiting: results of the Perugia consensus conference. Annals of Oncology 21 (Supplement 5):232-243 (2010). Updated in January 2013: http://www.mascc.org/assets/Guidelines-Tools/mascc_antiemetic_english_2014.pdf
L277: - Without known liver metastases, Aspartate aminotransferase (AST) and Alanine aminotransferase (ALT) \le 2.5 \times ULN
- With known liver metastases, AST and ALT \le 5.0 \times ULN
e) Serum Creatinine \le 1.5 \text{ m g/dL} (Standard units: \le 132.6 \mu\text{MOL/L}) or Creatinine Clearance \ge 60 \text{ mL/min}

- If a patient has a known hepatic or renal impairment, he/she may be enrolled in this study at the discretion of the Investigator, provided the inclusion criterion #8 items d) and e) are satisfied.
- If a patient has a known history or predisposition to cardiac conduction interval abnormalities, including QTc, he/she may be enrolled in this study at the discretion of the Investigator.
- Able to read, understand, follow the study procedure and complete patient diary.
L278: **Reviewer's Comments:** *The diagnostic (inclusion) criteria included adult chemotherapy-naïve male and female patients with a diagnosis of malignant solid tumor requiring a first treatment with one of the protocol pre-defined reference HEC regimens. It should be noted that the inclusion criteria did not include patients receiving anthracycline plus cyclophosphamide chemotherapy, which was consistent with the population studied to support intravenous palonosetron's approval for the HEC acute phase CINV indication.*
L279: ## Pertinent Exclusion Criteria
L280: - Lactating woman.
- Current use of illicit drugs or current evidence of alcohol abuse.
- Scheduled to receive moderately emetogenic chemotherapy or highly emetogenic chemotherapy from Day 2 to Day 5.
- Received or is scheduled to receive radiation therapy to the abdomen or the pelvis within 1 week prior to the start of the reference HEC administration on Day 1 or between Days 1 to 5.
- Any vomiting, retching, or nausea (grade \ge 1 as defined by National Cancer Institute) within 24 hours prior to the start of the reference HEC administration on Day 1.
- Symptomatic primary or metastatic CNS malignancy.
- Active peptic ulcer disease, gastrointestinal obstruction, increased intracranial pressure, hypercalcemia, an active infection or any illness or medical conditions (other than malignancy) that, in the opinion of the Investigator, may confound the results of the study, represent another potential etiology for emesis and nausea (other than chemotherapy-induced nausea and vomiting [CINV]) or pose unwarranted risks in administering the study drugs to the patient.
- Known hypersensitivity or contraindication to 5-HT3 receptor antagonists (e.g., palonosetron, ondansetron, granisetron, dolasetron, tropisetron, ramosetron) or dexamethasone.
- Known contraindication to the IV administration of 50 mL 5% glucose solution.
- Participation in a previous clinical trial involving palonosetron.
- Any investigational drugs (other than those given in this study) taken within 4 weeks prior to Day 1, and/or is scheduled to receive any investigational drug during the present study.
L281: - Systemic corticosteroid therapy at any dose within 72 hours prior to the start of the reference HEC administration on Day 1. However, topical and inhaled corticosteroids are permitted.
- Scheduled to receive bone marrow transplantation and/or stem cell rescue therapy.
- Any medication with known or potential antiemetic activity within 24 hours prior to the start of the reference HEC administration on Day 1, including:HT3 receptor antagonists (e.g., ondansetron, granisetron, dolasetron, tropisetron, ramosetron, palonosetron)
- NK1 receptor antagonists (e.g., aprepitant or any other new drug of this class)
- benzamides (e.g., metoclopramide, alizapride)
- phenothiazines (e.g., prochlorperazine, promethazine, fluphenazine, perphenazine, thiethylperazine, chlorpromazine)
- benzodiazepines (except if the subject is receiving such medication for sleep or anxiety and has been on a stable dose for at least seven days prior to Day 1)
- butyrophenones (e.g., haloperidol, droperidol)
- anticholinergics (e.g., scopolamine, with the exception of inhaled anticholinergics for respiratory disorders, e.g., ipratropium bromide)
- antihistamines (e.g., cyclizine, hydroxyzine, diphenhydramine, chlorpheniramine)
- domperidone
- mirtazapine
- olanzapine
- prescribed cannabinoids (e.g., tetrahydrocannabinol or nabilone)
- Over the Counter (OTC) antiemetics, OTC cold or OTC allergy medications
- Concurrent medical condition that would preclude administration of dexamethasone for 4 days such as systemic fungal infection or uncontrolled diabetes.
L282: **Reviewer's Comments:** *There were no unreasonable exclusion criteria with respect to laboratory values, lactation, prior trial participation, complicating comorbidities (i.e., CNS malignancy, chronic GI conditions, bone marrow transplantation), allergies/contraindications to study drug administration, or concurrent antiemetic medication use. The main characteristics of the population were similar to the target US population.*
L283: ## Dose Selection
L284: The approved dose of Aloxi (palonosetron) IV for adults of 0.25 mg was used.
L285: ## Dosing Schedule

L290: ## Randomization/Stratification
L291: With respect to assignment to treatment, patients were to be randomized per a 1:1 randomization ratio, stratified by country and sex. Treatment assignment was managed through a static central blocked randomization stratified by sex (male, female) and country. The randomization scheme was to be reproducible and prepared prior to study start via a computerized system by the IWRS (Interactive Web Response System) vendor.
L292: ## Blinding
L293: The blinding of the study medications was guaranteed using identical placebos to the respective active drugs (double-dummy technique). When palonosetron 0.25 mg was to be administered as 30-min IV infusion, a 30-sec IV bolus of placebo was to be administered (Group 1). Conversely, when palonosetron 0.25 mg was administered as a 30-sec IV bolus, a 30-min infusion of placebo was to be administered (Group 2). Therefore, each patient was to receive a 30-sec IV bolus and a 30-min IV infusion, one of which contained the active investigational medicinal product while the other was a placebo.
L294: No blinding was required for dexamethasone, which was administered at the same dose and posology in both treatment groups.
L295: The Investigator had the possibility to unblind the study treatment in case of an emergency, when he/she considered it essential to know what treatments the patient had received.
L296: ## Administrative Structure
L297: There was no data safety monitoring board (DSMB) for this study. Two contract research organizations (CRO) were involved in this study. PSI CRO AG, based out of Zug, Switzerland, was the clinical CRO, and [REDACTED] (b) (4), based out of [REDACTED] (b) (4), was the CRO for Statistics and Data Management (SDM). There were no adjudication committees. There is no suggestion that any third-party entity assessed for safety or efficacy during this study.

L302: (a) Day -1 was the day immediately preceding Day 1.

(b) Approximately 24 h after the first study drug administration (i.e., intravenous [IV] palonosetron or placebo bolus administration) on Day 1.

(c) 120 h after the first study drug administration on Day 1. If Day 6 was a holiday or a weekend day, Visit 4 could have been scheduled within the 2 forthcoming days.
